It verifies each commitment and signature, validates each US TIN's format, and writes the IRIS upload files, the IRS TIN Matching bulk file and full hard copies. The IRIS files are uploaded from a browser profile used for nothing else and destroyed afterward. Agents never hold the key or the output.
- **The envelope is checked during the year, not in January.** Format validation runs in a browser the payee controls, and the last four digits and the commitment are the client's own claims. So fresh envelopes are opened on a routine. `POST /api/admin/tax/envelopes/export` streams only the envelopes not yet checked, encrypted whole to the offline key; McKale opens them on the offline machine in a weekly sitting while enrollment is open; the tool verifies each commitment, validates each US TIN's format and compares the claimed last four; and `POST /api/admin/tax/envelopes/result` records `tin_format_verified` per form (true, or false with a reason, never a digit). A foreign TIN has no one format: formats differ by country and the tool can source no rule for each, so for a foreign TIN it verifies the commitment and that the value is not empty, and nothing more. A W-8BEN with no TIN whose open lines are all foreign source has nothing to open, and its flag is true by rule. A form that fails opens a `tax_hold` and a fresh form request (`tin_envelope_failed`). Until the flag is true, cumulative payments to that payee stay under `TIN_UNVERIFIED_PAY_MAX_CENTS`: for a W-9 filer never above the lowest reporting threshold of the payee's open lines less one cent, and for a W-8 filer zero on any US-source line and unlimited on a foreign-source one, on which no return and no withholding depends. A junk envelope from a modified client can therefore never carry a payee past the point where a return or backup withholding was due. A sitting more than 10 days overdue raises `tin_check_overdue`.
- **The key and the page are proven during the year, not in January.** The vault holds a canary envelope of a known test value under each active key version. A quarterly offline drill decrypts it and an administrator records the result (`POST /api/admin/tax/key-drill`); the same drill runs after every key rotation, before January 2, and on McKale's own envelope before any other payee enrolls (gate G7). After every deploy of the payments origin or of tax code the drill is end to end: a fresh test form is submitted through the deployed page under a staff test account, and that envelope is exported and opened. A stored canary proves the key; only a fresh submission proves the page, so a release that breaks the encryption step is found the day it ships and not after a year of unreadable forms. A missed drill raises `tax_key_drill_overdue`.
- Rotation is a fresh key pair, a version number (`TAX_KEY_ACTIVE`) and a pinned fingerprint; old envelopes stay readable offline with the old private key. Losing every copy of a private key loses no money. It does cost a filing: a payee who does not certify again is filed with a missing TIN and the hard copy of their form cannot be reproduced. The drill exists so that a failed copy is found while the other still works.
- What a full compromise of the Worker and its storage yields: ciphertext, salted commitments and last four digits. Recipient copies and every screen show `XXX-XX-1234`. A compromise of the page that encrypts would defeat all of it: script on that page could read the TIN before encryption and use the identity key while it is loaded for a signing act. That is why the page runs on the payments origin, which renders text others wrote only as text, under an enforced content security policy, with its bundle hash and its served policy header watched from outside (15.4). It is also why the main site's origin, which renders forums, profiles and listings, never reads an identity file, never holds a private key, and carries no TIN field and no signing control of any kind.
- First-year TIN status is `unverified`: IRS TIN Matching is open only to payers that have already filed. Diligence is format validation and the KYC name match. After the first filing season McKale enrolls, and the offline tool's bulk file sets `matched` or `mismatch`. A name variant is never retried automatically.

Addresses, legal names as certified, countries and dates of birth live in the `TaxVault` object, not in KV, and are served through a narrow view. Bank account numbers are stored nowhere in Eustress.

### 6.7 Thresholds and income character

Every accrual row, every lot and every payment line carries its `line` (`effort`, `value`) and the `character` the signed rules give that line for that year. The effort and value parts are kept apart on every row so the accountant's ruling can be applied to past rows without guessing. A certificate is not a property of a lot. One certificate governs a payment: the form on file when the payment is made, held in its tax snapshot and required by the send gate to be the form still on file (5.2). Thresholds for payments made in 2026: 2,000 USD for Form 1099-NEC and most of Form 1099-MISC; 10 USD for royalties (1099-MISC box 2); any amount when tax was withheld. For 2027: 2,100 USD. Gross amount and payment count per payee are both kept, so a Form 1099-K answer, if the accountant gives one, can be filed without a rebuild.

### 6.8 Year totals and year-end filing

- `ytd:{payee}:{year}:{form_seq}:{line}:{character}` holds gross paid, tax withheld, wire fees, net, payment count, returned cents and the payee's state, and the US-source part of gross and of returns (`us_gross_cents`, `us_returned_cents`), written from the payment lines' `us_cents` in the same put as the debit or the return. Form 1042-S and the Form 1042 worksheet read only those two. A return restores a payment by line (5.9), and within a line its foreign-source cents before its US-source cents, so `us_returned_cents` is the US part of what it restored. **The form sequence in the key is the payment's**, taken from its tax snapshot. A payee's status is judged on the documentation held when a payment is made, not when its cents accrued, and value rows are paid 120 days after they accrue, so every change of status straddles unpaid rows. A payee who moves from a W-8 to a W-9 in June has one total per certificate by payment date: what is paid while the W-9 is on file is on the Form 1099 and inside the `yagg` aggregate that tests the threshold and the backup withholding floor, whenever it was earned, and of what is paid under a W-8BEN, the US-source part is withheld on and reported on Form 1042-S, whenever it was earned, and the foreign-source part is reported nowhere (6.4). The split needs no rebuild. It is written in the same put that records a payment's bank debit, by the Arizona calendar date of the bank posting. No expiry.
- **Thresholds are tested on the aggregate.** `yagg:{payee}:{year}:{family}` is the per-payee total by reporting form, maintained in the same puts, with a `pending_cents` part for payments in flight. `payee_year_reportable` reads it. The withholding floor and the "payees over a threshold" report both use it, so two lines of 1,500 USD each, or two W-9 sequences after an address change, are one 3,000 USD payee.
- **A return takes back only the cash that came back.** A return reduces the gross and net of the year in which the payment's debit posted by `returned_cents` and nothing else, whenever the return posts; a return into a year already frozen, furnished or filed also sets that year's correction flag. The gross that stays is the withholding already taken, any wire fee and any unrecovered loss, so the tax withheld is never larger than the income it was withheld from. Returned cents on which no tax was taken are untagged, and their repayment is a fresh payment judged on the documentation then held. R12 states the same thing from the bank's side, per recipient and per payment year: the outflows that posted in that year, less the returns of those outflows whenever they posted, equal that year's gross less `netted_cents` less withheld less wire fee.
- **A wire fee credit is treated the same way.** A fee below the deduction, or none at all, returns cents that were already reported: the payment's gross and wire fee fall by the credit in the same write, and the cents are reported when they are paid, tagged `withholding_settled` only when the payment carried withholding (5.6, 5.7). A payee who earned 100.00 USD is reported at 100.00 USD.
- **Netting is payment.** A clawback netted from later earnings is those earnings applied to a debt. When the clawed-back payment was reported in an earlier year than the netting, the netted amount is added to the gross of the year in which it is netted, as a payment with no bank posting, under `netted_cents` on that year's `ytd:{payee}:{year}:...` row (the form on file on the netting date, and the line and character of the lot it consumed); the earlier year is never touched. Netting inside one year changes nothing. Because `netted_cents` moved no cash, R12 subtracts it. The accountant confirms both rules (section 18, item 9).
- Gross is reported; the wire fee is the payee's cost (the accountant confirms).
- **January 2:** the prior year is frozen as a draft (`taxyear:{Y}`). A pre-filing report lists: payees over a threshold by reporting form, from the aggregate; any payee with withholding; missing or expired forms; name mismatches against KYC; missing addresses; returned payments; payments flagged `tax_year_crossed`; and year totals against Mercury outflows less returns per recipient, each return counted in the year of its payment's debit (R12). A mismatch blocks the draft.
- **January 5 to 20:** payees see their own year statement and can raise a correction through a payee case.
- **By January 25:** recipient copies are ready and announced by a notice, and stay available through October 15. No copy is stored anywhere: each is rendered on request inside the payee's `TaxVault` object from the profile and the year totals, with the TIN truncated, and served to its owner on the payments origin after a signed challenge (purpose `tax_doc_read`). A bearer token alone reads none, and the same holds for the tax form on file (`/api/tax/form`), so a lifted token or a leaked session key yields no legal name, address, TIN digit or tax document. Payment amounts are a different matter and are stated in 5.11. Payees without e-delivery consent are listed for paper mailing.
- **Filing.** `tax_yearend.mjs` exposes one seam, `TaxFiler`, with `exportYear(year)`. The implementation is `iris_csv`: the offline tool writes IRIS Taxpayer Portal CSV files for the 1099 series and for Form 1042-S (100 returns a file, built against the tax year's own template with a fixture test) and McKale uploads them. One sitting a year. `POST /api/admin/tax/year/{Y}/filed` records the receipt id and date. Corrections follow the same path. How Form 1042 itself is filed at 10 or more returns is a question for the accountant (section 18, item 27). A vendor adapter can sit behind the same seam for TIN matching, state filings and mailed copies; it would receive TINs, so it exists only if McKale chooses it (decision D15).
- **Legal dates, shown for 2026 payments:** Form 1099-NEC to the IRS and to recipients, and Form 945, by February 1, 2027; Form 1099-MISC to recipients by February 1 and to the IRS by March 31, 2027; Forms 1042-S and 1042 by March 15, 2027, only if any US-source foreign payment was made. A year with no payout has no information returns, and no contributor was paid in 2026: the first contributor returns follow this calendar one year later, for 2027 payments, unless payouts begin and a return is owed sooner (gate G5).
- **States.** The report counts payees by state from the W-9 address. `state_rules.{year}.json` (per state: direct filing required, threshold, withholding duty) is empty until the accountant fills it. Eustress withholds no Arizona tax and never offers to, so nothing is filed with Arizona.
- **Eustress's own vendors.** Expense rows carry a vendor. The year-end report lists every vendor paid at or above the year's threshold from a Mercury account (card payments excluded), with whether a W-9 is on file (`vendor:{id}`, the document stored encrypted to the same offline key). The offline tool writes their Form 1099-NEC rows beside the contributors'.

### 6.9 Eustress's own taxes

- **Income tax and classification.** `entity_tax.{year}.json`, set with the accountant, holds the LLC's tax classification as an effective-dated list (a second member makes the LLC a partnership from that date, which changes the payer identity behind every form; section 9), the federal and Arizona estimated payment dates and a set-aside rate. The year-end tool reads the classification in force on each date. The admin view shows the set-aside target (rate times trailing profit) and subtracts it from "safe to move from Engine Expenses". `entity_tax_due` fires 14 days before each date. McKale pays; nothing is automated. Provision entries are posted with the adjusting-entry route. Decision D18.
- **Sales tax and VAT.** Where and whether tax is collected is `sales_regions.json` (3.6), set from the accountant's ruling on each product (Tickets as prepaid value, Delta, plans). No registration threshold shelters Eustress in the EU or the UK, where a seller established elsewhere owes VAT on consumer digital sales from the first one, or in Arizona, where Eustress is physically present. Until gate G9 closes, decision D26 sets whether paid checkout is offered to buyers there. Tax that Stripe collects sits in 2300 and never enters the split, and a refund takes its tax part back out (3.3), so 2300 ties to Stripe's tax report; while collection is off somewhere sales are open, the admin view carries the accrued exposure for that place.
- **Form 1099-K to Eustress.** Stripe may issue one on gross card volume. The books reconcile gross charges before refunds and fees to it.

### 6.10 Records and retention

Tax forms, TIN envelopes, year totals, payment records, accrual rows, lots, B-notice records, the payout authorization, terms acceptances, consents, screening decisions with their inputs, and the administrator act log carry no expiry and live in Durable Object storage, never in KV. They are exempt from account deletion: `handleAdminDeleteUser` seals the `TaxVault` object and leaves it, the payee's Treasury rows and the `kyc_facts` in place. Minimums the policy exceeds: 4 years after the due date of the last return a record supports; 6 years for ACH entry and authorization records; 10 years for sanctions records. The policy is 10 years after the last payment to that payee. KYC images of rejected and abandoned sessions get an R2 lifecycle rule set with counsel (section 19).

---

## 7. The fixed-IP relay

Mercury accepts a direct-send call only from an allowlisted IP, and Cloudflare Workers have no fixed egress address on any self-serve plan. The relay is one small service whose only job is to turn a signed, schema-checked request from the Worker into one of a fixed set of Mercury calls, under limits it enforces by itself.

### 7.1 Where it runs, and what it costs

One Fly.io Machine per environment (`eustress-bank-relay`, `eustress-bank-relay-staging`), in a Fly organization that holds nothing but the relay: `shared-cpu-1x`, 256 MB, one US region, an app-scoped static egress pair (one IPv4, one IPv6; both allowlisted at Mercury) and a 1 GB volume for the nonce store, the audit file, the day ledger, the first-seen records and the journal head list. Fly's daily snapshots of the volume are kept 14 days; 7.5 states what the relay does when the volume is lost anyway.

| Item | Per month |
|---|---|
| Machine, running full time | 2.19 USD |
| Static egress IPv4 (the IPv6 of the pair is free) | 3.60 USD |
| Volume, 1 GB | 0.15 USD |
| **One environment** | **5.94 USD** |
| Production and staging | 11.88 USD |

Reasons: no operating system to patch; secrets set by McKale with `fly secrets set`; the egress address survives redeploys. Set aside: Cloudflare dedicated egress (an Enterprise contract), AWS Lambda behind a NAT gateway (about 36.50 USD a month and the most moving parts), a plain VPS (a server holding a bank credential that someone must patch).

The service exposes no public port. A `cloudflared` process in the same Machine dials out, from a configuration file built into the image and a locally managed tunnel credential that is a relay secret (`TUNNEL_CREDENTIALS`), not from a dashboard-managed tunnel whose route anyone with Zero Trust edit rights could repoint. The Worker reaches it through a Workers VPC Service binding (`BANK_RELAY`). Workers VPC is in beta, so the fallback is written and tested: a Tunnel hostname behind an Access Service Auth policy. Neither the request signature nor the response signature (7.4) depends on the transport: a second connector on the tunnel can read requests in transit and can sign nothing. Creator webhook delivery uses the global `fetch`, and a test asserts no creator-supplied URL can reach the binding.

The service is a standard-library-only Node 22 program (`node:http`, `node:crypto`), in `infrastructure/relay/`, with `node:test` tests against a fake Mercury. Only McKale deploys it, from a reviewed commit, and before the allowlist is set he verifies the image digest that Fly's own API reports for the Machine, read from the deploy machine. The health route reports a digest as well, which proves nothing by itself: a hostile image answers with whatever digest it likes.

### 7.2 What it holds

| On the relay | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `MERCURY_SEND_TOKEN` | Custom token: direct send, recipients and invites, internal transfer. Created by McKale in the week of its first live use. Allowlisted to the relay's two addresses. It is an organization token: nothing Mercury documents confines it to one source account, so it can send from Engine Expenses and Checking as well as from Bliss Treasury (7.11, Q4) |
| `MERCURY_REQUEST_TOKEN` | Custom token: request-send-money only. Created by the second Mercury user in the week of the first wire, so McKale is the approver of every wire |
| `BANK_FP_KEY` | Key for bank fingerprints. Exists nowhere else |
| `RELAY_RESPONSE_KEY` | Ed25519 private key that signs every answer. Generated on the relay; its public half is pinned in the Worker's source by key id |
| `TUNNEL_CREDENTIALS` | The credential of the relay's own tunnel. Whoever holds it can stand a second connector on the tunnel |
| `WORKER_VERIFY_KEYS` | Public halves of the Worker's signing keys, by key id. Two ids may be valid at once, which is how the key rotates |
| `ALERT_WEBHOOK` | An alert channel that does not pass through the Worker |
| Plain settings | `MERCURY_BASE_URL` (production or sandbox, nothing else accepted), `EE_ACCOUNT_ID`, `BT_ACCOUNT_ID`, `RELAY_ENV`, `RELAY_ARMED`, the caps |

The Worker holds `RELAY_SIGNING_KEY` (an Ed25519 private key McKale generates locally) and `MERCURY_READ_TOKEN`. A relay compromise cannot forge a Worker request, because the relay holds only public keys. A Worker compromise can ask the relay for the calls below, inside its caps and checks; it yields no bank write credential. Whoever holds only the tunnel can read requests in transit (legal names, email addresses, recipient ids, amounts) and can produce no answer the Worker will act on.

### 7.3 The three calls, and the support routes

Three routes move or queue money. They run one at a time, under a single lock that spans all three. Everything not listed is 404.

| Route | Input (strict schema) | Mercury call, built by the relay | Output (whitelist) |
|---|---|---|---|
| `POST /v1/ach` | payment id, recipient id, bank fingerprint and its version, amount cents, period, memo code | A look-up of BT transactions whose note equals the payment id, and of send-money requests that are this payment's (recipient, amount and memo code, created at or after the relay's first ledger entry for this payment key); if neither exists, `POST /account/{BT}/transactions`, `paymentMethod: "ach"`, `idempotencyKey: eus-pay-{payment id}`, the payment id as `note` and the memo code in the external memo | outcome, bank transaction id or request id, status, estimated delivery |
| `POST /v1/wire-request` | payment id, recipient id, bank fingerprint and its version, amount cents, period, memo code | A look-up of send-money requests under the same rule; if none, `POST /account/{BT}/request-send-money`, `paymentMethod: "internationalWire"`, `chargeType: "ours"`, purpose `contractor`, same key | outcome, request id, status |
| `POST /v1/sweep` | installment id, amount cents | A look-up of BT credits and EE debits whose note equals `eus-sweep-{installment id}`; if none, `POST /transfer`, source EE, destination BT, same key and note | outcome, both transaction ids, status |

Support routes, none of which moves money:

| Route | Purpose | Output |
|---|---|---|
| `POST /v1/invite` | `POST /recipients/invites` with `sendEmail: false`, `requireTaxDocument: false`, the legal name, and `contactEmail` (the payee's verified address, checked as an address). It never accepts a recipient id. Idempotent by `invite_ref`: the relay lists invites whose notes equal that ref before creating one | invite id, onboarding URL, expiry |
| `POST /v1/invite-status` | Reads the invite and, once complete, the recipient. On first completion it computes the fingerprint, writes its first-seen record and posts the recipient (legal name, country and fingerprint tail; never a bank digit) to `ALERT_WEBHOOK` | status, recipient id, name, method, bank country, account type, last four, bank fingerprint with its version, first-seen time, and for a wire recipient the bank's name and SWIFT code |
| `POST /v1/wire-manifest` | Re-verifies the fingerprint of every pending request's recipient, on either rail, and posts a ready-to-approve manifest, with its expiry, to `ALERT_WEBHOOK`; until it expires, re-checks every `WIRE_LIVE_RECHECK_MINUTES` and posts the manifest void on any change | count |
| `POST /v1/anchor` | Receives the journal entry digests from the last anchored head to the last backed-up head, the hourly heartbeat, the Worker version id and the active tax key fingerprint. No Mercury call (7.7) | accepted, or `anchor_fork` |
| `POST /v1/announce` | Forwards an announcement of a fixed schema (`kind` from a closed list, an id, an effective time, cents) to `ALERT_WEBHOOK`. Rate-limited to 30 an hour | accepted |
| `GET /v1/health` | One harmless read per token | armed, environment, base URL kind, token health with the days since each token's last successful write, observed egress address, caps, today's totals, and the image digest as the image itself reports it |

Contract points:

- No route accepts an account id, a URL, a header to forward, a payment method, `duplicateCheckOverride` or free-form JSON for Mercury. The relay never sends a domestic wire, a check, a recipient edit or a recipient delete.
- **No caller text reaches Mercury or a recipient.** Memos and invite notes are built by the relay from fixed templates and validated tokens: a period (`2026-W46`, `2026-11-17`, `2026-11`), a memo code matching `EP-` and six hexadecimal digits, an `invite_ref` of fixed shape. A legal name on a wire comes from Mercury's own recipient record. Every id is checked as a UUID before it enters a path.
- **`contactEmail` is the payee's own verified address**, the one field of an invite that Mercury requires beside the name. Mercury sends it nothing at invite time (`sendEmail: false`). Whether mail Mercury later sends to it can lead to a change of bank details is asked of Mercury (Q7). A changed record fails the fingerprint check at every send and at every re-check of a request awaiting approval, and is then not paid. One window remains: an edit made between the last re-check of a request awaiting approval and McKale's approval. It is narrowed to `WIRE_LIVE_RECHECK_MINUTES` while a manifest is live, and closed only if a send-money request snapshots the bank details (Q7; 5.7).
- **Look-ups are exact.** The relay lists by date window and compares the note field character for character. It never uses a free-text search, so `eus-sweep-po_1` can never match `eus-sweep-po_12`.
- Amounts arrive as integer cents and are converted to Mercury's decimal dollars by exact integer formatting; a mutation test covers the conversion.
- A Mercury 409 on a repeated key, or a transaction found by the look-up, is returned as `replayed` with the existing transaction, after the relay has compared its recipient and amount with the request; a difference is returned as `mismatch`.
- A 400 is `bank_rejected` only when its body matches Mercury's documented validation shape and names a field. Every other 400, including the 24 hour duplicate refusal, is `unknown`. The answer carries Mercury's status code, so the Treasury knows Mercury received the request and ends automatic resends on it (5.6).
- **A stalled request dies.** The 60 second clock of 7.4 is checked when a request arrives and again, inside the lock, immediately before the Mercury call. A request that waited past it (a stalled Machine, a long queue behind the lock) is dropped with no Mercury call and its ledger intent is closed as expired, so a send never lands long after the Worker read Mercury and found nothing. The answer, if anyone still waits for it, is `unknown`.
- **Every answer is signed** (7.4), and every answer of a money route is one of a closed set: `accepted`, `replayed`, `mismatch`, `awaiting_approval`, `refused_recipient`, `refused_wait {reason, not_before}`, `refused_cap`, `disarmed`, `permission`, `bank_rejected`, `refused_bank` (sweeps) and `unknown`. `refused_recipient` is only ever a deleted recipient, a recipient with no `inviteId`, or a fingerprint mismatch. Every timing rule answers `refused_wait` with the time it clears.
- A Mercury answer the relay reads as an approval item (a send that became a request), or a request the look-up finds to be this payment's, is `awaiting_approval` with the request id. A request matches on recipient, amount and memo code, and only when it was created at or after the relay's first ledger entry for that payment key (the start of the token's period when the ledger lacks one). `approved` is a terminal status, so an old approved request of the same amount is never this payment. A body the relay does not recognise is `unknown`.

### 7.4 Authentication

Headers: `X-Eus-Key-Id`, `X-Eus-Time` (unix seconds), `X-Eus-Nonce`, `X-Eus-Sig`: an Ed25519 signature over

```
v1 \n {RELAY_ENV label} \n {method} \n {path} \n {time} \n {nonce} \n {sha256 of the body, hex}
```

The relay rejects a clock difference over 60 seconds (at receipt, and again immediately before any Mercury call, 7.3), an environment label that is not its own (a staging Worker can never drive the production relay), an unknown key id and a bad signature. Two things are kept apart:

- **The nonce is random, 128 bits, fresh for every request**, and guards against replay of a captured request. The relay keeps seen nonces for 10 minutes on the volume, so a restart does not forget, and rejects a repeat. How long it keeps them has no bearing on a legitimate resend, which always carries a fresh nonce.
- **The payment key is in the body** and is what makes a money route idempotent. A request whose payment key the relay or Mercury has seen always takes the look-up-then-send path and answers with the existing transaction, at one minute or at thirty.

The relay sets Mercury's `Authorization` header itself and rebuilds each request from the schema; unknown fields are an error. The Treasury treats a request the relay rejected as `unknown`.

**Answers are signed too.** Requests to the relay are authenticated by the Worker's key; answers from it are authenticated by the relay's own. Every answer carries `X-Eus-Resp-Key-Id` and `X-Eus-Resp-Sig`, an Ed25519 signature by `RELAY_RESPONSE_KEY` over

```
v1-resp \n {RELAY_ENV label} \n {path} \n {request nonce} \n {status} \n {sha256 of the response body, hex}
```

The public half is pinned in the Worker's source by key id, with a test, and two ids may be valid at once for rotation. `relay_client.mjs` verifies the signature before it reads a field. An answer with no signature, a wrong signature or another request's nonce is `unknown` on a money route and a refusal on a support route: no onboarding link is shown, no fingerprint is stored, no manifest counts as issued. The tunnel is transport only. Someone who obtains the tunnel credential or repoints the tunnel receives signed requests and can answer nothing: not an onboarding URL from another Mercury organization, and not an invented `accepted`.

### 7.5 The relay's own checks and caps

The relay's limits bind against the one adversary they exist for: a Worker that sends many requests at once. Three things make them real.

- **One lock.** The three money routes share one mutex. A burst of fifty concurrent requests is fifty requests in a row, each seeing the ones before it.
- **A ledger written first.** Before any Mercury call the relay appends the intent (route, key, cents, recipient) to a durable day ledger on the volume; afterward, the outcome. Every cap figure is the larger of the ledger's total, counting intents with no recorded outcome, and Mercury's own list, counting pending and in-flight items. A list that lags a just-created transaction cannot hide it, and wiping the volume cannot reset a cap, because Mercury's list still counts.
- **Defined periods.** Daily caps apply to the UTC calendar day and, with the same figures, to the rolling 24 hours, so nothing doubles across midnight. Weekly and monthly rules count by the period token the request carries and the relay writes into the memo (`2026-W46`, `2026-11-17`, `2026-11`), never by the date a transaction posts or a request is approved, so the relay's period and the Treasury's are the same label. The relay accepts a token only for the current period or the one before it, so a caller cannot invent periods to multiply payments.

Before `/v1/ach` and `/v1/wire-request` the relay checks, independent of the Worker: armed; recipient active and created through an invite (a recipient with no `inviteId` is refused); recipient age; fingerprint equal to the one recomputed from Mercury's live record; the per-recipient rules; amount within the per-payment cap; the day's total and count within the daily caps; BT balance sufficient. Before `/v1/sweep`: amount within its caps and no larger than EE's available balance. Any Mercury read failure refuses the send. A cap refusal answers `refused_cap`, which the Treasury treats as "pause and alert", never as "retry". A timing rule answers `refused_wait` with the time it clears, which the Treasury treats as "wait, then try again or return the cents to the lots", never as a fault on the destination.

**Recipient age without a Mercury timestamp.** Mercury's recipient object carries no creation or modification time. The age is therefore the later of two facts the relay can prove: the invite's `createdAt`, read through the recipient's `inviteId`; and the relay's own first-seen record of `{recipient id, bank fingerprint}`, written when it first computed that fingerprint and posted the recipient to `ALERT_WEBHOOK`. A recipient is payable 72 hours after that post. A changed fingerprint is a different record and starts again.

**A lost volume does not restart the clock for proven recipients.** A recipient whose first-seen record is missing is assumed neither new nor old. The relay looks, in order, for proof of its age: the volume restored from a snapshot; Mercury's own history (a transaction to this recipient, posted more than 72 hours ago, whose routing details produce the current fingerprint proves the fingerprint at that age); and a restore file McKale builds from the recipient announcements in the out-of-band channel and loads on the Machine himself, accepted per entry only when the invite's `createdAt` is not later than the announcement and the announced fingerprint tail equals the current one. Only a recipient with none of these is written at that moment and waits 72 hours, and the answer for it is `refused_wait {too_young}`, never a recipient fault. `first_seen_missing` is raised either way. The head list is rebuilt from the daily digests, which carry it, and the day ledger from Mercury's own lists, which the caps already read. The runbook is in 15.2.

**Per-recipient rules, from Mercury's own history.** These hold even when the Worker is the attacker, because the relay reads them itself:

- The first payment to a recipient is at most `CAP_FIRST_ACH_CENTS` or `CAP_FIRST_WIRE_CENTS`, and no second payment goes to it until the first has been posted for the return watch period with no linked return.
- One ACH per recipient per period token (`CAP_ACH_RECIPIENT_PERIOD` names the token kind: the ISO week under weekly cadence, the day under daily cadence, the month under monthly cadence) and one wire per recipient per month token. A transaction or request carrying the same payment id is the same payment, never a second one. A W46 payment sent on the Monday of W47 is W46's and does not use W47; a November wire approved in December is November's. The health route reports the token kind, and the Worker's preflight refuses to start a run when it differs from `ACH_CADENCE` (`cadence_mismatch`).
- At most `CAP_FIRST_PAYMENTS_DAY` (5) first payments a day.
- For wires, open send-money requests count toward the monthly cap and toward the per-recipient rule, by the month token in their memo, and a second open request for one recipient is refused.
- A send-money request that is an ACH payment's own (recipient, amount and memo code, created since that payment's first attempt) is that payment waiting for approval, never grounds for a second send. Any other open request for an ACH recipient counts against that recipient's period like a transaction, by the period token in its memo and against the current period when it carries none, so the send waits (`refused_wait {period_used}`) and R4 flags the stray request.

A compromised Worker that enrolls its own recipients through the invite route therefore meets, with no help from the Treasury: an announcement of each one to McKale's channel, 72 hours of waiting after it, a first payment of 25.00 USD, five such payments a day, and nothing further to any of them for a week.

**Caps sized to the honest system.** The Treasury sends ACH once a week under `RUN_MAX_CENTS`; daily caps alone would let a hostile Worker send that much every day, about seven times the honest throughput, for as long as Bliss Treasury lasted. So the relay also enforces `CAP_ACH_WEEK_CENTS` per ISO week and `CAP_ACH_30D_CENTS` per rolling 30 days, sized under the weekly cadence to one and to five runs of `RUN_MAX_CENTS`. It refuses ACH outside the configured run day and its catch-up window (`ACH_RUN_WEEKDAY`, `ACH_RUN_WINDOW_DAYS`) with `refused_wait {outside_run_window}`. And it **disarms itself** when the first payments or the recipients first seen in an ISO week exceed `SELF_DISARM_MULTIPLE` (3) times the trailing median of the last eight weeks, above a floor (`SELF_DISARM_FLOOR`, 10, which McKale raises for a launch cohort he expects): `RELAY_ARMED` goes false until he re-arms it, with an immediate message to `ALERT_WEBHOOK` (`relay_self_disarmed`). A Worker that enrolls recipients at a quiet pace and then pays them inside every per-recipient rule still meets one run's worth a week in total.

**The caps follow the cadence.** `ACH_RUN_WEEKDAY`, `CAP_ACH_WEEK_CENTS` and `CAP_ACH_30D_CENTS` are set together with `ACH_CADENCE`, in one change, each sized to what the honest system sends under that cadence:

| `ACH_CADENCE` | `ACH_RUN_WEEKDAY` | `CAP_ACH_WEEK_CENTS` | `CAP_ACH_30D_CENTS` |
|---|---|---|---|
| `weekly` | The run's weekday | One run of `RUN_MAX_CENTS` | Five runs |
| `daily` | `any` (every business day) | Five runs | Twenty-two runs |
| `monthly` | `month:{day}` | One run | Two runs, since 30 days can hold two month starts |

The health route reports the run schedule and both caps. The Treasury's preflight refuses to start a run when any of them does not fit `ACH_CADENCE` and `RUN_MAX_CENTS` (R27, `cadence_mismatch`), so the relay never refuses a payment the cadence allows and never allows more than the cadence sends.

Starting caps, raised only by McKale with `fly secrets set`: ACH 500 USD per payment and 25 USD for a first payment; 2,000 USD and 50 payments a day; 2,000 USD per ISO week and 10,000 USD per rolling 30 days. Wire requests 2,000 USD each and 100 USD for a first wire; 10,000 USD a month. Sweep 5,000 USD per transfer, 10,000 USD a day. Invites 50 a day, and 0 while `ENROLLMENT_MODE` is `off`. The daily digest states the caps in force, so a cap change is visible on the out-of-band channel. A per-token daily limit at Mercury, as an outer wall the relay cannot be talked past, is asked for in section 21 (Q4).

Response hygiene: Mercury returns full account and routing numbers on recipients and on transaction details. The relay reduces every Mercury response to its whitelist before anything is logged or returned. A test with a fake Mercury that returns account numbers fails if one reaches a log line or a response.

### 7.6 Kill switches, fastest first

1. **Halt** in the Treasury: any finance administrator, or a tripwire. Seconds. Stops every payee send and every funding sweep at the source and freezes the clock of every delayed act that releases money. A halt of scope `all` also stops the top-up and the clock of every other delayed act except the unhalt (8.6).
2. **`PAYOUTS_MODE=off` and `SWEEP_MODE=off`**: a Worker deploy by McKale.
3. **`RELAY_ARMED=false`**, or stopping the Machine: McKale, one command. Works if the Worker is compromised. A disarmed relay refuses every route except health, anchor and announce, which call Mercury for nothing: no payment, no wire request, no sweep, no invite.
4. **Deleting both Mercury write tokens, or removing the addresses from their allowlists**: McKale, in Mercury. Works if the Worker and the relay are both compromised. Final.

No switch reaches what is already in flight, so the runbook (15.2) adds two acts in Mercury itself: cancel the pending transactions the outflow checks named, on any of the three accounts, before their cutoff, and reject every pending send-money request.

### 7.7 Audit log, digest, witness and heartbeat

- One JSON line per request, to the volume and to stdout: time, request id, route, key, amount, recipient id, outcome, Mercury status code, Mercury transaction id. The key is the opaque payment id, which names no account and no rail. Never a token, an account or routing number, or a name.
- The Treasury writes its own `rl:{ts}:{id}` for every relay call and stores the relay's receipt on each `pay:` and `sweep:` record. A daily check compares the two counts and digests (`relay_audit_mismatch`).
- To `ALERT_WEBHOOK`, without passing through the Worker: an immediate message for every recipient first seen, every refusal by a cap, every fingerprint change on a pending request, and every announcement; the ready-to-approve manifest on request; and a daily digest of counts, totals, caps in force, the active tax key fingerprint, the Worker version id and the journal head, each digest carrying the hash of the one before. A recipient announcement carries the legal name, the country and the fingerprint tail, never a bank digit. This channel is the one place outside the Worker where the roster can be read in the clear, by design, and its account is held like a credential (15.4).
- **A roster compared outside the Worker.** A Worker that runs hostile code defeats every check that runs in the Worker: it writes a matching `pay:` record for each payment it steals, and R3, the Treasury and the Cashbook all read clean. It writes the roster too. The roster McKale exports (`roster/export`) is the Worker's own account of whom it paid, so agreement between Mercury and that roster proves only that Mercury agrees with the Worker. Three things stand outside it. The relay's weekly digest lists outflows per recipient id with the week's and the 30 days' totals against their caps. The relay announced every recipient to McKale on the day it first saw it. And the offline machine keeps every earlier month's roster, which no later Worker can rewrite. At each month close McKale downloads the month's Bliss Treasury transaction export from the Mercury dashboard on an ordinary machine, a read he already has, and carries the file to the offline machine with the encrypted roster, the way every export travels. The offline tool holds no Mercury credential, and the offline machine never goes online with the tax key loaded (6.6). The tool reads the file, compares Mercury's outflows per recipient with the month's roster, and lists every recipient that is new this month and every recipient paid more than `ROSTER_GROWTH_MULTIPLE` (3) times its trailing three month mean. **The names are the Worker's.** The legal name the relay announces, and the one a wire manifest shows, is the name the Worker supplied in `/v1/invite`, and the customer id on a roster line is the Worker's choice too. A hostile Worker can enroll a mule account under a real subscriber's name and customer id, so comparing an announcement with a paid invoice compares two values the attacker picked and proves nothing by itself. The month close therefore rests on two facts the Worker cannot choose. The offline tool keeps the map of customer id to recipient across every roster it has seen, and lists a customer with a second live recipient and a recipient whose customer changed. And for the month's new recipients McKale sends one notice from his own mailbox, blind-copied, for each listed customer, to the `customer_email` on that customer's earliest paid Creator, Professional or Bundle invoice dated before the recipient's enrollment: "A bank account was enrolled for Eustress payouts on your account this month. If that was not you, reply to this message." Stripe fixes that field when it finalizes the invoice, and the Worker's key has no write on invoices, so neither hostile code nor a billing portal session it opens can change where the notice goes. The customer's current billing email is never the address: the key can open a billing portal session for any customer (3.4), and a portal can let its user change that email. McKale's offline tool, or his reading of the Dashboard, lists as `roster_mismatch` every listed customer whose current billing email differs from that invoice's. McKale also turns off customer information updates in the portal's default configuration (17.2, item 11); a key that may write portal sessions may be able to write portal configurations too, so that narrows the path and is not the defence. The Worker can neither write the notice nor suppress it. The month does not lock until the notice has gone out and McKale has read each listed line against the relay's announcement and that customer's paid Creator, Professional or Bundle invoice (locked decision 8 means every real payee has one). An outflow to a recipient the roster lacks, an amount above the roster's, a flagged customer, a reply that says "not me", or a line he cannot confirm is `roster_mismatch` (R25). Stated plainly: against hostile code inside the Worker the caps bound the loss to the honest system's own throughput, and what finds it is a person, once a week in the digest and once a month at the close. A mule enrolled under a real subscriber is found by that subscriber's reply. A mule for which the attacker bought a plan of its own, with an address the attacker reads, is found by nothing and bounded by the caps, and it costs a paid plan and a card for each.
- **The relay is an append-only witness of the journal.** Each anchor call carries the digests of the entries from the last anchored sequence number to the last backed-up head: the `anchor` stream runs `backup_step` to completion first and sends no digest past the journal head that backup's manifest names (15.3). The relay's head is therefore never ahead of the newest backup, and a restored journal always contains everything the relay has witnessed. An anchor with nothing new to send is still the heartbeat. The relay recomputes the chain from the head it stored itself and accepts the batch only if it links; otherwise it refuses, keeps its head and posts `anchor_fork`. It keeps its head list on the volume and in the digest. Code execution in the Worker can rewrite the journal and every checkpoint the Worker verifies; it cannot make the relay's chain accept a history that does not extend the one the relay already holds. The offline replay tool takes the relay's head list as an input and the monthly close runs it (8.5).
- **Dead-man's switch.** The Worker's hourly tick calls `/v1/anchor`. If the relay hears nothing for 3 hours it sends `worker_silent` to `ALERT_WEBHOOK`. If the Worker cannot reach the relay for 3 ticks it raises `relay_unreachable`. Each side reports the other's silence.
- Mercury's own payment notifications to McKale are a third, independent trail and are switched on.

### 7.8 Token health

Mercury deletes a token unused for 45 days and downgrades one whose write permission goes unused for 45 days. Which writes count is undocumented, so nothing relies on a keepalive, and no token is created long before its first write: the send token in the week the live sweep starts, the request token in the week of the first wire. Until then the relay runs with none: its announce, anchor and health routes call Mercury for nothing, and every money route answers `disarmed`. Sweeps and weekly ACH keep the send token in use; the request token is used monthly. The health route reads with each token daily and reports the days since each token's last successful write; `bank_token_idle` fires at 30. A 401 or 403 on a write raises `bank_token_degraded` and pauses the rail that token serves: a refusal of the request token pauses wires and leaves ACH and sweeps running; a refusal of the send token halts. The runbook: create a token with the same allowlist, `fly secrets set`, check health. Mercury's 7 day warning email is in the runbook.

### 7.9 Environments

The staging relay is its own app with a sandbox token and `RELAY_ENV=staging`. The base URL has exactly two permitted values. Staging never holds a production Mercury token. The Worker refuses `MERCURY_ENV=production` anywhere but production, and a relay whose reported environment differs from the Worker's trips a halt. Staging inherits production's crons, so every mode is `off` there by default and unset Mercury account ids fail closed.

### 7.10 The Worker's read token

`MERCURY_READ_TOKEN` needs no allowlist and is used straight from the Worker for accounts, transactions, events, send-money requests (for ACH payments as well as wires), recipient invites by id, and auto-transfer rules. Reading from the Worker is deliberate: the unmatched-outflow check must not depend on the relay it is watching. `mercury_read.mjs` applies a field allowlist (`id`, `status`, `amount`, `kind`, `createdAt`, `postedAt`, `estimatedDeliveryDate`, `failedAt`, `reasonForFailure`, `feeId`, `relatedTransactions`, `requestId`, `counterpartyId`, `counterpartyName`, `externalMemo`, `note`, `trackingNumber`, `bankDescription`, `resourceVersion`) and drops `details`, every routing block and every recipient body before any log line or write. The Worker never requests a recipient object.

Stated plainly: Eustress never stores, logs or requests bank account numbers, and the claim stops there. The read token itself can read recipient objects and transaction details that carry routing and account numbers, so a Worker compromise can read them from Mercury. McKale creates the token as a Custom token with the narrowest read scopes Mercury offers; whether a scope without recipient bank details exists is an open question to Mercury (section 21). Decision D16 records the alternative of proxying reads through the relay and why it is not the default.

### 7.11 Residual risk, stated plainly

If the relay host itself is taken over, the attacker holds a send token on an allowlisted address and the relay's caps do not bind them. The token is not confined to Bliss Treasury: it can create a recipient with typed details and send from Engine Expenses, which holds every Stripe payout before its sweep, and from Checking, as easily as from Bliss Treasury. The same is true of anyone who holds the credential that deploys the relay: a Fly token that can deploy an image or run a command in the Machine can read the send token from an allowlisted address. Section 15.4 treats those credentials as bank credentials for that reason. What still stands against a taken relay: Mercury's own per-account limits; the outflow checks in the Worker, which cover all three accounts and read pending debits as well as posted ones, so that within one poll of a payment being created, while it can still be cancelled, an unexpected debit on Bliss Treasury halts and alerts (R3), and one on Engine Expenses or Checking alerts with the Mercury ids to cancel and is booked to Suspense, never as an expense (R5), and no expense rule explains a payment sent to a recipient, whatever name the recipient carries, so a theft with the send token cannot dress itself as a vendor or as the IRS (8.4); the relay's own weekly and 30 day caps where the attacker holds the Worker and not the relay; Mercury's notifications; the deploy tripwire, which reads the image digest from Fly's own API and so sees a hostile release; kill switch 4. A command run inside the Machine changes no digest and leaves nothing the tripwire can read: for that path the outflow checks are the detection. Reducing this further is a question for Mercury (a per-token daily limit; a send token limited to Bliss Treasury as the source of external sends, with Engine Expenses usable only as the source of an internal transfer; whether a custom token can be barred from creating recipients with typed bank details) and for an insurer. It is the largest single exposure in the design and the reason the relay is kept this small.

---

## 8. Reconciliation, alerts and the admin view

### 8.1 Ingestion

All reads use read credentials. Every provider read, every R2 write and every KV write of the finance system happens inside the `Cashbook` or the `Treasury` object, on that object's own alarm, in metered steps. The Worker's hourly `scheduled()` invocation only wakes them (8.9).

- **Every `BANK_POLL_MINUTES` (10; the Cashbook alarm, re-armed by the hourly tick if it ever stops):** Mercury `GET /events` from the stored cursor, applied by `resourceVersion`, never by arrival order; the quiescent wall read for EE, CHK and BT (2.4); the pending-outflow check on all three accounts (R3, R5); statuses of payments in flight, from the transaction list and the send-money request list; the gate re-check of payments awaiting approval; the replay sweep of unapplied rows. A 429 doubles the interval up to 60 minutes and raises `bank_poll_slowed`; the detection window of the outflow checks is one poll interval, whatever it is.
- **Hourly:** Stripe payouts not yet composed; open wire requests; run triggers; `unbooked:` retries; dispute watch records; the anchor and heartbeat; the staff digest and overdue check.
- **Daily, 6:00 AM Arizona time:** full reconciliation; the Stripe wall; Stripe balance-settings drift; Mercury auto-transfer rules; relay health and audit comparison; sanctions list sync; token health; `verify_step`; meters. The book backup does not wait for this hour: it runs after every run step and every sweep landing, before every anchor, and at the end of each day (15.3).
- **Mercury webhook** (`POST /api/mercury/webhook`, PUBLIC; HMAC-SHA256 over `{t}.{raw body}` with the endpoint secret, constant-time compare, 5 minute window, answer within 5 seconds): only enqueues the resource id. The poll is the source of truth, because Mercury webhooks retry for about a day, can be disabled automatically and do not exist in the sandbox.

Every tick writes its result (`tick:{YYYY-MM-DDTHH}`), not only the midnight one.

### 8.2 Checks

| # | Check | When | On failure |
|---|---|---|---|
| R1 | Coverage of Bliss Treasury (2.4), with unexplained debits counted in full against cover | Every determinate wall; before every run | Halt, scope `payouts`; the top-up keeps running |
| R2 | Bank wall for BT at a quiescent read (2.4) | Every poll | A determinate, unexplained shortfall on two consecutive polls: halt, `bt_shortfall`. A difference explained by tracked pending rows: `wall_class_unset`, no halt. Surplus: `bt_surplus`. `BANK_WALL_INDETERMINATE_MAX` indeterminate polls in a row: halt, `wall_indeterminate` |
| R3 | Every outflow from BT matches a `pay:` record, a wire fee, a Mercury fee, or an expected-outflow record (a planned repayment, a planned carry return, a planned Suspense refund or transfer to Checking). The check reads tracked pending debits as well as posted ones: a pending outflow whose note matches no record is caught while it can still be cancelled. Posting rules are unchanged; a pending row still posts nothing | Every poll | Halt; `unmatched_outflow` with the Mercury transaction ids to cancel. A debit pulled by an outside party: `external_debit`, whose text carries the dispute deadline (about two banking days for a business account). The check fires again on every poll while an unmatched row remains. An acknowledgement suppresses nothing: the halt lifts only when the row has been cancelled at Mercury, matched, or charged to carry by the delayed act of 8.6 |
| R4 | Two outflows carrying one payment id; two open send-money requests for one recipient on either rail; any open request for an ACH recipient that no `awaiting_approval` payment explains; a posted debit whose note names a payment in `void`, `failed` or `returned` state (it posts to Suspense); a relay answer of `mismatch` (the transaction it names posts to Suspense as an unmatched debit, since its recipient or amount is not its payment's) | Every poll | Halt; `duplicate_payment` |
| R5 | Bank walls for EE and CHK at a quiescent read; every posted Mercury transaction has an applied `mt:` record (a pending row is tracked, never applied); and every debit on EE or CHK, pending or posted, is a sweep leg, a Stripe debit, an own transfer, a planned outflow, a debit the IRS or EFTPS pulled within the open deposit worksheets, or a card charge or vendor-pulled debit that an `exprule` covers within its ceiling. A payment sent from either account to a recipient (an outgoing ACH, wire, check or real-time payment, which is all the send token can create) is explained by a plan made first (`xo:`) and by nothing else: no rule covers one | Every poll | Unknown rows to that account's Suspense, never to an expense account. A debit that fits none of those: `unmatched_outflow` at first sight, with the Mercury transaction ids to cancel, repeated each day while the row is unexplained. No halt: a halt stops nothing on those accounts, and the remedy for a theft is kill switch 4. A determinate, unexplained wall difference alerts |
| R6 | Stripe wall | Daily; each payout | Unknown rows to Suspense; `stripe_unmatched` |
| R7 | Stripe tie-out per payout | Each payout | No sweep; `stripe_tieout_failed` |
| R8 | Every Stripe payout matched to a bank credit within its window: on EE, or where it landed, under `pobank:{po_id}` with `bank_account` BT or CHK (4.3 S4) | Hourly | No sweep of that payout; `stripe_payout_unmatched` |
| R9 | Every confirmed payout swept within 2 business days (while `SWEEP_MODE` is `live`). Cents deferred for dispute cover are excluded while they are deferred and counted from the day their entry is removed | Daily | `sweep_overdue` |
| R10 | Clearing: nothing in 1130 older than 1 business day; no installment `submitted` beyond a day; no payment `submitted` beyond its window; 2009 zero outside a settlement; no item in 2050 older than 10 business days (`wire_fee_missing`); no item in 2410 older than `REVERSAL_CLEARING_MAX_DAYS`; no reversal whose cash side posted that long ago and that no payout carries; nothing in 1210 older than 60 days (`disputed_debit_stale`) | Hourly | `clearing_stuck` |
| R11 | Wire fee charged equals fee deducted | Per wire | `wire_fee_mismatch` |
| R12 | Per recipient and per payment year: the Mercury outflows that posted in that year, less the returns of those outflows whenever the returns posted, equal that year's totals' gross less `netted_cents` less withheld less wire fee, plus the part of any stray payment posted in that year that the payee's payable did not cover (8.6). A return counts in the year of its payment's debit, the year whose totals it reduced (5.6), and `netted_cents` moved no cash | Monthly; before filing | Blocks the year-end draft |
| R13 | The book identity; every mapped bucket equals its account; payee rows sum to `owed_cents`; per payee, rows and lots equal the owed part and holds equal the held part; the conservation identity; the transit identity; `pending_reversal_cents` recomputed from the `rev:` records in bounded steps; both Suspense parts of BT non-negative; recoverable equals receivable; every `dep:` and `rev:` has its `jref:`; no legacy state key | Daily, and in `verify_step` | Halt, scope `all`; `subledger_mismatch` |
| R14 | Journal verify from the last checkpoint; the relay's witness accepts each anchor; the monthly offline replay against the relay's head list | Daily; each anchor; month close | Halt, scope `all`; `journal_broken`, `anchor_fork` |
| R15 | EE holds at least the unswept share plus the computed floor; auto-transfer rules | Daily; before a sweep | `ee_below_unswept` with the safe-to-move figure |
| R16 | Stripe: balance below zero, a negative payout, settings drift, a changed external account, any Connect transfer | Per event; daily | `stripe_negative`, `stripe_settings_drift` |
| R17 | Relay audit agrees with the `rl:` log; the anchor heartbeat is fresh | Daily; hourly | `relay_audit_mismatch`, `relay_unreachable` |
| R18 | Unit meters: Tickets and Delta issued against the deposits | Daily | `meter_drift`; no stop |
| R19 | Withholding against each deposit clock, and the next-day threshold; under-withheld tax still open ahead of the Form 1042 due date | Daily | `withholding_deposit_due`, `withholding_next_day`, `nra_underwithheld` |
| R20 | Month close: the posted journal against Stripe balance transactions and all three Mercury accounts | Monthly | The month stays open |
| R21 | Freshness: sanctions list age and acceptance, relay and token health, days since each token's last write, the last success of each scheduler stream, no unapplied provider row (posted and not applied) older than 1 hour, the tax key drill, the offline envelope check's last sitting, the deploy tripwire's last report including the payments origin's bundle hash and the policy headers it read from the live payments origin and the live main site (`origin_policy_missing`), a session-key overlap with no end (`session_overlap_open`), the new session key still unset after the step 6 release (`session_key_unset`), creation records written while `CHECKOUT_RECORDS_SINCE` is unset (`checkout_records_open`), next year's tax rules not activated from December 1 (`tax_rules_missing`) | Hourly | Named alert each |
| R22 | Every wire awaiting approval still passes the send gate and its snapshot; the relay's fingerprint re-check | Every poll; hourly on the relay, and every 5 minutes while a manifest is live | `wire_do_not_approve` on both channels |
| R23 | The finance administrator set in force equals the configured list | Daily; on change | `admin_set_changed` on both channels |
| R24 | Stop-only acts, bans of enrolled payees, unsigned destination removals and signed administrator reads, each against its daily limit | Per act | `stop_act_tripwire`, `ban_tripwire`, `dest_removal_tripwire`, `admin_read_bulk` on both channels |
| R25 | Outside the Worker: the relay's per-recipient digest; the offline month-close comparison of Mercury outflows per recipient, read from the dashboard export McKale carries to the offline machine, with the month's roster and with the earlier rosters kept offline; the offline map of customer id to recipient across every roster; McKale's monthly notice, from his own mailbox, to the email on each new recipient's customer's earliest paid plan invoice dated before the enrollment, with every such customer whose current billing email differs from it listed, and his reading of each new recipient and each jump. The roster and every announced name are the Worker's own account, so agreement among them alone proves nothing (7.7) | Weekly; month close | `roster_mismatch`; the month stays open |
| R26 | Every Stripe payout's `destination` equals `STRIPE_EE_BANK_ID`, or the payout has been matched where it landed and its part funded (4.3 S4); Stripe holds no balance of another type | Each payout; daily | `stripe_settings_drift`; the payout is packed into no sweep until it is matched. Once matched and funded it is green and does not hold the month open |
| R27 | The book's clocks agree with the relay's: no `refused_wait` answer; the relay's period token kind, run schedule and weekly and 30 day caps fit `ACH_CADENCE` and `RUN_MAX_CENTS` (7.5) | Per answer; run preflight | `relay_wait_disagreement`; `cadence_mismatch` and the run does not start |

### 8.3 Alerts

One finance alert path, separate from takedowns. Recipients are `FINANCE_ALERT_MAILBOX` plus every finance administrator, from one exported `staffAddresses`. Every alert is `required`, has a stable `alertId` per run or per day, and is one message per run or per day, never one per payment. Each alert also appears in the admin view and in `/api/admin/cron-health`, whose fields describe bank payouts, sweeps and holds. No person approves daily, so this path is the only human signal: every automatic stop raises one.

Kinds: `settlement_not_started`, `settlement_phase_unknown`, `relay_unreachable`, `relay_disarmed`, `relay_audit_mismatch`, `anchor_fork`, `bank_token_degraded`, `bank_token_idle`, `bank_poll_slowed`, `bt_shortfall`, `bt_surplus`, `wall_indeterminate`, `unmatched_outflow`, `external_debit`, `duplicate_payment`, `subledger_mismatch`, `journal_broken`, `clearing_stuck`, `run_waiting`, `run_anomaly`, `cap_reached`, `wires_awaiting_approval`, `wire_approval_overdue`, `wire_do_not_approve`, `wire_details_mismatch`, `payout_returned`, `payout_trace_requested`, `payee_dispute`, `bank_compliance_hold`, `outcome_unknown`, `wire_fee_mismatch`, `wire_fee_missing`, `fx_fee_unrecovered`, `stripe_payout_failed`, `stripe_payout_unmatched`, `stripe_tieout_failed`, `stripe_unmatched`, `stripe_negative`, `stripe_settings_drift`, `suspense_item`, `ee_below_unswept`, `large_deposit_waiting_cover`, `sweep_overdue`, `sweep_carry_stale`, `sanctions_hit`, `sanctions_list_stale`, `sanctions_sync_rejected`, `ofac_report_due`, `ofac_annual_report_due`, `tax_form_review`, `tax_key_mismatch`, `tax_key_drill_overdue`, `tax_reask_tripwire`, `bnotice_deadline`, `withholding_deposit_due`, `withholding_next_day`, `tax_rules_missing`, `entity_tax_due`, `dispute_rate_high`, `meter_drift`, `margin_below_floor`, `investment_unmatched`, `uncategorized_at_close`, `first_seen_missing`, `deploy_drift`, `admin_set_changed`, `halt_set`, `unhalt_pending`, `admin_act_pending`, `admin_act_cancelled`, `wall_class_unset`, `bt_available_mismatch`, `disputed_debit_stale`, `relay_wait_disagreement`, `cadence_mismatch`, `relay_self_disarmed`, `ach_awaiting_approval`, `roster_mismatch`, `stop_act_tripwire`, `ban_tripwire`, `payee_banned`, `dest_removal_tripwire`, `dest_conflict`, `dest_conflict_repeat`, `admin_read_bulk`, `session_overlap_open`, `wallet_value_over_limit`, `tin_envelope_failed`, `tin_check_overdue`, `origin_policy_missing`, `checkout_records_open`, `nra_underwithheld`, `session_key_unset`.

### 8.4 The expense ledger

One expense ledger. Every posted debit on Engine Expenses or Checking that is not a system movement and whose counterparty has a rule is, in one posting keyed `mt:{mercury_txn}`, an expense row in integer cents with vendor, bank date, account and Mercury transaction id: Dr 60xx, Cr 1100 or 1110. Checking carries card activity, so the posted-rows rule matters here most: a card authorization is tracked in the Cashbook while it is pending and posts nothing; it posts once, at the amount that settles; and an amount that changes after posting is a reversing entry and a corrected one (2.2). The category comes from administrator-maintained rules, and a rule is narrow on purpose. `exprule:{key}` maps one payer of a debit Eustress did not send to an account and a vendor, with a ceiling: a card merchant, by the merchant Mercury states on the card transaction, or a vendor that pulls a debit, by Mercury's `counterpartyId` where the row carries one and by its name only where it carries none. A debit above the rule's `max_cents` is not covered. **No rule explains a payment Eustress sent.** A payment sent from Engine Expenses or Checking to a recipient (an outgoing ACH, wire, check or real-time payment, read from the transaction's `kind`; an unknown kind counts as sent) is the only thing the relay's send token can create on those accounts, and whoever creates it chooses the recipient's name. It is explained by a plan made first and by nothing else: `POST /api/admin/expenses/plan` writes an expected-outflow record (`xo:`, kind `vendor_payment`) for an exact amount, an account, a vendor and an expiry, and the matching debit posts as that plan says (`vendpay:{id}`). A recipient named after a vendor that has a rule, or named IRS, is covered by no rule. A debit nothing covers is not an expense yet. It posts to that account's Suspense (2910 or 2920) and raises `unmatched_outflow` the moment it is first seen, pending or posted, with the Mercury transaction id (R5). A debit nobody has named is what a theft through the relay's send token looks like, and booking it as a cost would tie the wall on stolen money. A new card or pulled-debit vendor therefore costs McKale one alert and one rule: `PUT /api/admin/expenses/rules`, when it takes effect, reclasses the Suspense debits that rule covers to its account (`mtc:{txn}`). A vendor he pays by transfer costs a plan the day before, or one alert and one signed classification of that payment afterward (8.6). The bound that remains is stated plainly: a debit pulled under a vendor's own counterparty and under that rule's ceiling is booked as that vendor's cost, and the vendor's invoice at the month close is what catches it. A debit that was a theft and that the bank did not return is classified to 6095 with its dispute reference. Account 6999 holds only what a rule or an administrator put there for want of a category, and `POST /api/admin/expenses/categorize` moves it at month close. Three kinds of debit are never an expense at apply. A debit matched to a record planned first (an investor return, a planned outflow) posts as that record says. An internal transfer between Eustress's own accounts is an own transfer through 1130 between Engine Expenses and Checking, and, toward Bliss Treasury with no relay installment behind it, Suspense on both sides until `sweeps/manual` settles it as an installment of that exact amount or the own-transfer classification of 8.6 settles it as carry. And a debit whose counterparty is the IRS or EFTPS posts to that account's Suspense until it is classified as a withholding deposit (6.5) or as Eustress's own tax, so a withheld amount is never both a liability and an expense. It raises no alert only when the IRS or EFTPS pulled it and its amount is no greater than the open deposit worksheets. A larger one, an estimated tax payment that no plan announced, and any payment sent to a recipient named IRS alert like any other debit. Stripe's fees come from balance transactions; bank and wire fees from Mercury's fee transactions. An owner-paid or card-paid cost that never touches a Mercury account is posted with the adjusting-entry route.

Categories: Cloudflare, servers and the relay, domain, tools, AI model vendors (xAI, Anthropic, TypeSafe, Workers AI), bank and wire fees, professional fees, tax filing, taxes, labor (real payments only), other. Costs have this one source: no cost table is typed in by hand and none is hard-coded. The review-cost meter is an estimate outside the ledger; its daily figures are what each AI invoice is checked against.

### 8.5 Posted, lockable books

- `books.mjs` reads the posted journal. `GET /api/admin/books/close?month=` returns the month's trial balance, its balance sheet (cash at Stripe, cash in each Mercury account, owed to contributors, held, withholding payable, investor principal, the reserve) and the journal as totals by account and day. It carries no entry line and no `payee` field; entry detail is the export's, which is a signed read (8.8).
- `POST /api/admin/books/lock` locks a month once 2009 and 6999 are zero as of the month's last day; every Suspense posting (2900 to 2930) and every leg through 1130 dated in the month has its exit posted, at any date, so an item that straddles the month end (a sweep whose two legs post a business day apart, a charge row reclassed the next morning) holds only its own month and a later month's items hold no earlier one; a reclass (`mtc:`, `sbtc:`) is dated when it posts, in an open month, and names the item it exits by `relates_to`; every check is green for the month's last day; and the offline replay of that month against the relay's head list has been recorded. A locked month refuses postings. A late refund, a released hold or a fee true-up is a dated entry in the open month with `relates_to` naming the original.
- `POST /api/admin/books/adjust` posts a balanced adjusting entry (`adjje:{id}`): accruals, depreciation, reclasses, provisions, vendor payables through 2600, costs paid outside Mercury. One allowlist governs it, the one in 2.6; an account that is not on the list cannot be touched, whichever section a reader starts from.
- `GET /api/admin/books/export?from=&to=&format=csv` hands the accountant the trial balance and the general ledger detail (date, entry, account, debit, credit, memo, ref), with opaque payee references in place of account ids. It is a signed read (8.8).
- The public statement is monthly and has two honest names: `accrued_usd` (declared to named contributors) and `disbursed_usd` (left the bank), with `returned_usd` beside them. It follows the monthly rule of 5.1 (two significant digits, both groups at least `PUBLIC_K_MIN`, a minimum change of the enrolled set) and carries no per-day figures and no contributor counts. It is cut in the settlement of the month's first score date, with the fold of 5.1. The Rust structs in `economics_live.rs` match it.
- The book is backed up to the private bucket `FINANCE_BACKUPS`, which holds nothing else, under `book-backups/{date}/`, encrypted (15.3).

### 8.6 Halt, unhalt and delayed administrator acts

**Who is a finance administrator.** `FINANCE_ADMINS` is deploy configuration: a fixed list of account ids, each with the fingerprint of its Ed25519 public key. `requireFinanceAdmin` accepts a request only when the session's account is on the list and the key held by that account's `AccountAuth` object has the listed fingerprint. A KV flag or a role on a KV user record grants nothing on any finance route; a test asserts 403 on every one for an account that has only the flag. A change of the list is a deploy by McKale and raises `admin_set_changed` on both channels. The administrator finance page is served from the payments origin (15.4), and its signing control loads the key for one act and drops it.

**Halt.** `halt {on, scope, causes [{kind, ref, at}], by, at, unhalt_at}` lives in the book state. It is set at once by `POST /api/admin/finance/halt` or by a tripwire. A halt has a scope, because a halt that also stops its own remedy can never be lifted.

| Scope | Set by | Stops | Leaves running |
|---|---|---|---|
| `payouts` | R1, R2, R3, R4, a run anomaly; an administrator's halt by default | Every payee send. Every sweep that funds a pool or reserve part. The clock of every delayed act that releases money: a hold `pay`, a void, a payment record, a carry return, a repayment plan | Accrual, ingestion and reconciliation. A top-up installment (4.5), which can only add cover. The clock of the unhalt. And the clocks of the acts that can only add cover, explain a Suspense item or clear a cause: `suspense/classify`, `sweeps/manual`, `suspense/charge-debit`, `withholding/remitted`, `runs/{id}/ack-anomaly` |
| `all` | R13, R14, a refusal of the send token, a relay whose environment or base URL does not match the Worker's; an administrator's halt with `scope: "all"` | Everything above, the top-up, and the clock of every delayed act except the unhalt | Accrual, ingestion and reconciliation. The clock of the unhalt |

The unhalt's own clock runs under a halt of either scope; it is judged against `causes` when it takes effect. A cause that arrives while an unhalt is pending is appended to `causes`, and the pending unhalt is judged against the whole list. A carry that Mercury's fees pushed past its bound is therefore topped up while payouts stay halted, the classification that explains a Suspense item finishes its clock during the halt it would otherwise deadlock with, and a run anomaly is acknowledged under the halt it set. That acknowledgement only marks one run: no payment moves until the unhalt has taken effect.

**The default for every act.** Every route under `/api/admin/finance`, `/api/admin/tax`, `/api/admin/books`, `/api/admin/expenses`, `/api/admin/investments`, `/api/admin/sanctions` and `/api/admin/treasury` that is not a GET is **signed, delayed and announced**. The request carries a fresh Ed25519 signature by the administrator's key over the act and its arguments. It is written to the Treasury's own hash-chained act log (`act:{seq}`; the KV audit entry is a copy, since a KV record can be deleted with one command). It takes effect as an `adminq:{id}` record after the delay, is announced (`admin_act_pending`) to every staff address and, through the relay's `/v1/announce`, on the out-of-band channel, can be cancelled by any finance administrator, and counts against `ADMIN_DAY_MAX_CENTS` when it sends an amount toward a payee. Lifting a hold, undoing an act and classifying a Suspense item carry no such amount. Three kinds of act carry no delay, and each of them is still signed and logged. Acts that can only stop money: halt, the cancel of a pending act or of an unhalt, and the setting of a legal or estate hold. Three acts that move nothing and decide nothing: asking the relay for a manifest, recording a key drill, and starting the settlement the tick would start. And the three exports (`tax/envelopes/export`, `tax/year/{Y}/export`, `finance/roster/export`): each bundle is encrypted whole to the offline key and is built and streamed in the answer to the signed request itself, because a delayed act takes effect on an alarm with nobody to stream to, and holding a bundle until it is fetched would be the stored copy that never exists. An export is announced on both channels and counted against `ADMIN_READ_DAY_MAX`, and what it yields to a stolen key is ciphertext that only the offline machine opens. A route-policy test holds that list, walks every finance route and fails on any other exception.

**Stop-only acts are counted.** An act that can only stop money can still do harm in bulk: a stolen key that sets a legal hold on every payee stops every payout until each hold is lifted. More than `STOP_ACT_DAY_MAX` (5) legal holds and estate holds signed in one day raises `stop_act_tripwire` on both channels and puts every further one that day behind the administrator delay. Halt and cancel are never limited. Confirming a sanctions match as blocked is not a stop-only act: `sanctions_hold` has already stopped the money, and the block, which starts a federal reporting clock on a named person, is a delayed act with an `unblock` behind it (5.5).

**Bulk undo.** `POST /api/admin/finance/acts/undo` reverses everything one key fingerprint signed since a stated time: its queued acts are cancelled, the holds it set are lifted, the block requests it queued are withdrawn. It is one delayed, signed, announced act and it is outside the daily cents total, so recovery from a stolen key takes one morning and not weeks of daily limits.

**The delay.** With one administrator, the delay is the only bar a stolen key meets, and one hour elapses while McKale sleeps. So while decision D19 is open, an act takes effect at the first 9:00 AM Arizona time on a business day that is at least `ADMIN_DELAY_MINUTES` (720) after it was queued. When D19 names a second person, that person receives every announcement and holds the cancel; the delay is then McKale's to set, never under 60 minutes.

**An act is judged when it takes effect.** The delay opens a gap between the moment an act's facts were checked and the moment it applies. So the write that applies an `adminq` act re-runs its whole precondition and its bank-fact binding, with a fresh provider read wherever the binding names one: a void re-reads the transaction list and the request list; `sweeps/manual`, `withholding/remitted` and `suspense/classify` re-read their rows; a sanctions clear screens against the list current at that hour. A failed re-check cancels the act and raises `admin_act_cancelled`; an act is never applied on a stale read. The same write requires the signing key's fingerprint to be in `FINANCE_ADMINS` as deployed at that moment, and a deploy that removes a key purges every act that key queued. A key McKale has removed can make nothing fire.

**Lifting a halt** is one of these acts: `POST /api/admin/finance/unhalt` needs the signature, waits the delay (its clock runs under the halt it lifts), is announced on both channels with the list of every delayed act that will resume its clock, and carries a cancel link (`POST /api/admin/finance/unhalt/cancel`). A tripwire halt also needs its cause acknowledged in the request, a determinate bank wall under 26 hours old, and, at the moment it takes effect, every cause in `causes` to be false. False has one meaning for each cause:

| Cause | False when |
|---|---|
| R1, coverage | The coverage statement holds at the latest determinate wall |
| R2, shortfall | The latest determinate wall shows no unexplained shortfall |
| R2, indeterminate walls | A determinate wall has been read since |
| R3, unmatched outflow | No unmatched row remains: each was cancelled at Mercury, matched, or charged to carry by `suspense/charge-debit` |
| R4, duplicate | No flagged item remains unexplained: every duplicate, closed-payment or mismatched debit was cancelled at Mercury, booked as a stray payment to its payee, or charged to carry as a debit in dispute, and no recipient has two open requests or an open request that no `awaiting_approval` payment explains. A payment the flag left in `review` may stay there: review is a state of one payment and needs no halt |
| A run anomaly | The acknowledgement of that run id has been applied, or the run has ended with its unsent payments back in the lots |
| R13 | `verify_step` and R13 have passed in full since the halt |
| R14 | The journal verifies from the last checkpoint and the relay has accepted an anchor since the halt; after an `anchor_fork`, only after the restore of 15.3.
